<div style="line-height: 1.6em; font-weight: bold;"><div>Influences</div></div>
<ul class="mw-collapsible-content" style="margin-top: 0; margin-bottom: 0; line-height: inherit; list-style: none; margin-left: 0;"><li style="line-height: inherit; margin: 0"><div class="center" style="width:auto; margin-left:auto; margin-right:auto;"><a href="/wiki/Aristotle" title="Aristotle">Aristotle</a>, <a href="/wiki/Empedocles" title="Empedocles">Empedocles</a>, <a href="/wiki/Heraclitus" title="Heraclitus">Heraclitus</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gaius_Musonius_Rufus" title="Gaius Musonius Rufus">Musonius Rufus</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Plato" title="Plato">Plato</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pythagoras" title="Pythagoras">Pythagoras</a>, <a href="/wiki/Socrates" title="Socrates">Socrates</a></div></li></ul>
</div></td></tr><tr class="note"><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data"><div class="collapsible-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed" style="text-align: left;">
<div style="line-height: 1.6em; font-weight: bold;"><div>Influenced</div></div>
<ul class="mw-collapsible-content" style="margin-top: 0; margin-bottom: 0; line-height: inherit; list-style: none; margin-left: 0;"><li style="line-height: inherit; margin: 0"><div class="center" style="width:auto; margin-left:auto; margin-right:auto;">Virtually all of subsequent <a href="/wiki/Christian_philosophy" title="Christian philosophy">Christian philosophy</a> and Catholic theology.</div></li></ul>
</div></td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Justin Martyr</b> (<a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span lang="el">Ἰουστῖνος ὁ μάρτυς</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek" title="Romanization of Greek">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Greek-language romanization"><i lang="el-Latn">Ioustinos ho Martys</i></span>; <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;AD 100</span>&#160;– c.<span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;AD 165</span>), also known as <b>Justin the Philosopher</b>, was an early <a href="/wiki/Christian_apologetics" title="Christian apologetics">Christian apologist</a> and <a href="/wiki/Philosophy" title="Philosophy">philosopher</a>.
</p><p>Most of his works are lost, but two apologies and a dialogue did survive. The <i><a href="/wiki/First_Apology_of_Justin_Martyr" title="First Apology of Justin Martyr">First Apology</a></i>, his most well-known text, passionately defends the morality of the Christian life, and provides various ethical and <a href="/wiki/Philosophy" title="Philosophy">philosophical</a> arguments to convince the Roman emperor, <a href="/wiki/Antoninus_Pius" title="Antoninus Pius">Antoninus</a>, to abandon the persecution of the Church. Further, he also indicates, as <a href="/wiki/Augustine_of_Hippo" title="Augustine of Hippo">St. Augustine</a> would later, regarding the "true religion" that predated Christianity,<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> that the "seeds of Christianity" (manifestations of the <a href="/wiki/Logos_(Christianity)" title="Logos (Christianity)">Logos</a> acting in history) actually predated Christ's <a href="/wiki/Incarnation_(Christianity)" title="Incarnation (Christianity)">incarnation</a>. This notion allows him to claim many historical Greek philosophers (including <a href="/wiki/Socrates" title="Socrates">Socrates</a> and <a href="/wiki/Plato" title="Plato">Plato</a>), in whose works he was well studied, as <a href="/wiki/Virtuous_pagan" title="Virtuous pagan">unknowing Christians</a>.
</p><p>Justin was <a href="/wiki/Christian_martyr" title="Christian martyr">martyred</a>, along with some of his students, and is venerated as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Catholic_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Catholic-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>  the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Churches" title="Oriental Orthodox Churches">Oriental Orthodox Churches</a>, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.lcms.org/worship/church-year/commemorations">Lutheran Churches</a>, and in <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglicanism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li><a href="/wiki/Thurible#Byzantine_Rite" title="Thurible">Use of incense</a></li></ul>
</div></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content">

</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar" title="Template:Eastern Orthodox Church sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar" title="Template talk:Eastern Orthodox Church sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Eastern_Orthodox_Church_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table><figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Engraving_of_Justin_Martyr_-_2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/Engraving_of_Justin_Martyr_-_2.jpg/220px-Engraving_of_Justin_Martyr_-_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="287" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/Engraving_of_Justin_Martyr_-_2.jpg/330px-Engraving_of_Justin_Martyr_-_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/Engraving_of_Justin_Martyr_-_2.jpg/440px-Engraving_of_Justin_Martyr_-_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="584" data-file-height="762" /></a><figcaption>A bearded Justin Martyr presenting an open book to a Roman emperor. Engraving by <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Callot" title="Jacques Callot">Jacques Callot</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Justin Martyr was born around AD 90-100,<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:0_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> into a <a href="/wiki/Greeks" title="Greeks">Greek</a> family,<sup id="cite_ref-:0_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> at Flavia Neapolis (today <a href="/wiki/Nablus" title="Nablus">Nablus</a>) near the ancient biblical city of <a href="/wiki/Shechem" title="Shechem">Shechem</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Samaria" title="Samaria">Samaria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Palestine_(region)" title="Palestine (region)">Palestine</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> He knew little or no <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a> and <a href="/wiki/Aramaic" title="Aramaic">Aramaic</a>, and had only a passing acquaintance with <a href="/wiki/Judaism" title="Judaism">Judaism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_10-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> His family may have been pagan, since he was uncircumcised, and defined himself as a <a href="/wiki/Gentile" title="Gentile">Gentile</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> His grandfather, Bacchius, had a Greek name, while his father, Priscus, bore a Latin name,<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>  which has led to speculations that his ancestors may have settled in Neapolis soon after its establishment or that they were descended from a Roman "diplomatic" community that had been sent there.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the opening of the <i>Dialogue</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-Dialogue_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dialogue-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Justin describes his early education, stating that his initial studies left him unsatisfied due to their failure to provide a belief system that would afford theological and metaphysical inspiration to their young pupil. He says he tried first the school of a <a href="/wiki/Stoicism" title="Stoicism">Stoic</a> philosopher, who was unable to explain God's being to him. He then attended a <a href="/wiki/Peripatetic_school" title="Peripatetic school">Peripatetic</a> philosopher but was put off because the philosopher was too eager for his fee. Then he went to hear a <a href="/wiki/Pythagoreanism" title="Pythagoreanism">Pythagorean</a> philosopher who demanded that he first learn music, astronomy, and geometry, which he did not wish to do. Subsequently, he adopted <a href="/wiki/Platonism" title="Platonism">Platonism</a> after encountering a Platonist thinker who had recently settled in his city. 
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>And the perception of immaterial things quite overpowered me, and the contemplation of ideas furnished my mind with wings, so that in a little while I supposed that I had become wise; and such was my stupidity, I expected forthwith to look upon God, for this is the end of Plato's philosophy.<sup id="cite_ref-Dialogue_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dialogue-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Some time afterwards, he chanced upon an old man, possibly a Syrian Christian,<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> in the vicinity of the seashore, who engaged him in a dialogue about God and spoke of the testimony of the prophets as being more reliable than the reasoning of philosophers.
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>There existed, long before this time, certain men more ancient than all those who are esteemed philosophers, both righteous and beloved by God, who spoke by the Divine Spirit, and foretold events which would take place, and which are now taking place. They are called prophets. These alone both saw and announced the truth to men, neither reverencing nor fearing any man, not influenced by a desire for glory, but speaking those things alone which they saw and which they heard, being filled with the Holy Spirit. Their writings are still extant, and he who has read them is very much helped in his knowledge of the beginning and end of things, and of those matters which the philosopher ought to know, provided he has believed them. For they did not use demonstration in their treatises, seeing that they were witnesses to the truth above all demonstration, and worthy of belief; and those events which have happened, and those which are happening, compel you to assent to the utterances made by them, although, indeed, they were entitled to credit on account of the miracles which they performed, since they both glorified the Creator, the God and Father of all things, and proclaimed His Son, the Christ [sent] by Him: which, indeed, the false prophets, who are filled with the lying unclean spirit, neither have done nor do, but venture to work certain wonderful deeds for the purpose of astonishing men, and glorify the spirits and demons of error. But pray that, above all things, the gates of light may be opened to you; for these things cannot be perceived or understood by all, but only by the man to whom God and His Christ have imparted wisdom.<sup id="cite_ref-Dialogue_16-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dialogue-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Moved by the aged man's argument, Justin renounced both his former religious faith and his philosophical background, choosing instead to re-dedicate his life to the service of the Divine. His newfound convictions were only bolstered by the ascetic lives of the early Christians and the heroic example of the <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyrs</a>, whose piety convinced him of the moral and spiritual superiority of Christian doctrine. As a result, he thenceforth decided that the only option for him was to travel throughout the land, spreading the knowledge of Christianity as the "true philosophy."  His conversion is commonly assumed to have taken place at Ephesus<sup id="cite_ref-J._Quasten,_p.196-7_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-J._Quasten,_p.196-7-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> though it may have occurred anywhere on the road from <i>Syria Palestina</i> to Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mosaic_of_St._Justin_Martyr,_Mount_of_the_Beatitudes.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Mosaic_of_St._Justin_Martyr%2C_Mount_of_the_Beatitudes.jpg/220px-Mosaic_of_St._Justin_Martyr%2C_Mount_of_the_Beatitudes.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="188" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Mosaic_of_St._Justin_Martyr%2C_Mount_of_the_Beatitudes.jpg/330px-Mosaic_of_St._Justin_Martyr%2C_Mount_of_the_Beatitudes.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Mosaic_of_St._Justin_Martyr%2C_Mount_of_the_Beatitudes.jpg/440px-Mosaic_of_St._Justin_Martyr%2C_Mount_of_the_Beatitudes.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1231" data-file-height="1053" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Mosaic" title="Mosaic">Mosaic</a> of the beheading of Justin Martyr</figcaption></figure>
<p>He then adopted the dress of a philosopher himself and traveled about teaching.  During the reign of <a href="/wiki/Antoninus_Pius" title="Antoninus Pius">Antoninus Pius</a> (138–161), he arrived in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> and started his own school. <a href="/wiki/Tatian" title="Tatian">Tatian</a> was one of his pupils.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> In the reign of <a href="/wiki/Marcus_Aurelius" title="Marcus Aurelius">Marcus Aurelius</a>, after disputing with the <a href="/wiki/Cynicism_(philosophy)" title="Cynicism (philosophy)">cynic</a> philosopher <a href="/wiki/Crescens_the_Cynic" title="Crescens the Cynic">Crescens</a>, he was denounced by the latter to the authorities, according to Tatian (Address to the Greeks 19) and Eusebius (HE IV 16.7–8).  Justin was tried, together with six friends (two of them slaves educated by him; Euelpistus and Hierax), by the urban prefect <a href="/wiki/Junius_Rusticus" title="Junius Rusticus">Junius Rusticus</a>, and was <a href="/wiki/Decapitation" title="Decapitation">beheaded</a>. Though the precise year of his death is uncertain, it can reasonably be dated by the prefectoral term of Rusticus (who governed from 162 and 168). The martyrdom of Justin preserves the court record of the trial.<sup id="cite_ref-J._Quasten,_p.196-7_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-J._Quasten,_p.196-7-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>The Prefect Rusticus says: Approach and sacrifice, all of you, to the gods. Justin says: No one in his right mind gives up piety for impiety. The Prefect Rusticus says: If you do not obey, you will be tortured without mercy. Justin replies: That is our desire, to be tortured for Our Lord, Jesus Christ, and so to be saved, for that will give us salvation and firm confidence at the more terrible universal tribunal of Our Lord and Saviour. And all the martyrs said: Do as you wish; for we are Christians, and we do not sacrifice to idols. The Prefect Rusticus read the sentence: Those who do not wish to sacrifice to the gods and to obey the emperor will be scourged and beheaded according to the laws. The holy martyrs glorifying God betook themselves to the customary place, where they were beheaded and consummated their martyrdom confessing their Saviour.<sup id="cite_ref-Catholic_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Catholic-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Relics">Relics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Relics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The church of St. John the Baptist in <a href="/wiki/Sacrofano" title="Sacrofano">Sacrofano</a>, a few miles north of Rome, claims to have his relics.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Jesuits,_Valletta" title="Church of the Jesuits, Valletta">Church of the Jesuits</a> in Valletta, Malta, founded by papal decree in 1592 also boasts relics of this second century Saint.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A case is also made that the relics of St. Justin are buried in Annapolis, Maryland. During a period of unrest in Italy, a noble family in possession of his remains sent them in 1873 to a priest in Baltimore for safekeeping. They were displayed in St. Mary's Church for a period of time before they were again locked away for safekeeping. The remains were rediscovered and given a proper burial at St. Mary's, with Vatican approval, in 1989.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:S._Iusti_M..jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/S._Iusti_M..jpg/220px-S._Iusti_M..jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/S._Iusti_M..jpg/330px-S._Iusti_M..jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/S._Iusti_M..jpg/440px-S._Iusti_M..jpg 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="2448" /></a><figcaption>Relics of St. Justin and other early Church martyrs can be found in the lateral altar dedicated to St. Anne and St. Joachim at the Jesuit's Church in Valletta, Malta.</figcaption></figure>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1882 <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_XIII" title="Pope Leo XIII">Pope Leo XIII</a> had a <a href="/wiki/Mass_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mass (Catholic Church)">Mass</a> and an <a href="/wiki/Liturgy_of_the_Hours" title="Liturgy of the Hours">Office</a> composed for his <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a>, which he set at 14 April,<sup id="cite_ref-Catholic_6-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Catholic-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> one day after the date of his death as indicated in the Martyrology of Florus; but since this date quite often falls within the main <a href="/wiki/Easter" title="Easter">Paschal</a> celebrations, the feast was moved in 1968 to 1 June, the date on which he has been celebrated in the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Rite" title="Byzantine Rite">Byzantine Rite</a> since at least the 9th century.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Justin is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Lesser_Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Lesser Festival (Anglicanism)">Lesser Festival</a> on 1 June.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Iustini_Philosophi_et_martyris_Opera.tif" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Iustini_Philosophi_et_martyris_Opera.tif/lossless-page1-220px-Iustini_Philosophi_et_martyris_Opera.tif.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="358" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Iustini_Philosophi_et_martyris_Opera.tif/lossless-page1-330px-Iustini_Philosophi_et_martyris_Opera.tif.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0c/Iustini_Philosophi_et_martyris_Opera.tif/lossless-page1-440px-Iustini_Philosophi_et_martyris_Opera.tif.png 2x" data-file-width="3738" data-file-height="6086" /></a><figcaption><i>Iustini Philosophi et martyris Opera</i> (1636)</figcaption></figure>
<p>The earliest mention of Justin is found in the <i>Oratio ad Graecos</i> by his student <a href="/wiki/Tatian" title="Tatian">Tatian</a> who, after calling him "the most admirable Justin", quotes a saying of his and says that the Cynic Crescens laid snares for him.  <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> speaks of Justin's martyrdom and of Tatian as his disciple. Irenaeus quotes Justin twice<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> and shows his influence in other places.  <a href="/wiki/Tertullian" title="Tertullian">Tertullian</a>, in his <i><a href="/wiki/Adversus_Valentinianos" title="Adversus Valentinianos">Adversus Valentinianos</a></i>, calls Justin a philosopher and a martyr and the earliest antagonist of <a href="/wiki/Christian_heresy" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian heresy">heretics</a>. <a href="/wiki/Hippolytus_(writer)" class="mw-redirect" title="Hippolytus (writer)">Hippolytus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Methodius_of_Olympus" title="Methodius of Olympus">Methodius of Olympus</a> also mention or quote him.  <a href="/wiki/Eusebius_of_Caesarea" class="mw-redirect" title="Eusebius of Caesarea">Eusebius of Caesarea</a> deals with him at some length,<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> and names the following works:
</p>
<ol><li>The <i><a href="/wiki/First_Apology" class="mw-redirect" title="First Apology">First Apology</a></i> addressed to <a href="/wiki/Antoninus_Pius" title="Antoninus Pius">Antoninus Pius</a>, his sons, and the Roman Senate;<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>A <i><a href="/wiki/Second_Apology_of_Justin_Martyr" title="Second Apology of Justin Martyr">Second Apology of Justin Martyr</a></i> addressed to the Roman Senate;</li>
<li>The <i>Discourse to the Greeks</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> a discussion with Greek philosophers on the character of their gods;</li>
<li>An <i><a href="/wiki/Exhortation_to_the_Greeks" title="Exhortation to the Greeks">Hortatory Address to the Greeks</a></i><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> (known now not to have been written by Justin);<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>A treatise <i>On the Sovereignty of God</i>, in which he makes use of pagan authorities as well as Christian;</li>
<li>A work entitled <i>The Psalmist</i>;</li>
<li>A treatise in scholastic form <i>On the Soul</i>; and</li>
<li>The <i><a href="/wiki/Dialogue_with_Trypho" title="Dialogue with Trypho">Dialogue with Trypho</a></i>.</li></ol>
<p>Eusebius implies that other works were in circulation; from <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">St Irenaeus</a> he knows of the apology "Against Marcion," and from Justin's "Apology"<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> of a "Refutation of all Heresies".<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Epiphanius_of_Salamis" title="Epiphanius of Salamis">St Epiphanius</a><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Jerome" title="Jerome">St Jerome</a><sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> mention Justin.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Tyrannius_Rufinus" title="Tyrannius Rufinus">Rufinus</a> borrows from his <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> original of <a href="/wiki/Hadrian" title="Hadrian">Hadrian</a>'s letter.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Spurious_medieval_works">Spurious medieval works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Spurious medieval works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After Rufinus, Justin was known mainly from St Irenaeus and Eusebius or from spurious works. A considerable number of other works are given as Justin's by <a href="/wiki/Arethas_of_Caesarea" title="Arethas of Caesarea">Arethas of Caesarea</a>, <a href="/wiki/Photios_I_of_Constantinople" title="Photios I of Constantinople">Photius of Constantinople</a>, and other writers, but this attribution is now generally admitted to be spurious. The <i>Expositio rectae fidei</i> was assigned by Johann Dräseke to <a href="/wiki/Apollinaris_of_Laodicea" title="Apollinaris of Laodicea">Apollinaris of Laodicea</a>, but it is probably a work of as late as the 6th century. The <i><a href="/wiki/Exhortation_to_the_Greeks" title="Exhortation to the Greeks">Cohortatio ad Graecos</a></i> has been attributed to Apollinaris of Laodicea, Apollinaris of Hierapolis, as well as others. The <i>Epistola ad Zenam et Serenum</i>, an exhortation to Christian living, is dependent upon <a href="/wiki/Clement_of_Alexandria" title="Clement of Alexandria">Clement of Alexandria</a>, and was assigned by <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Batiffol" title="Pierre Batiffol">Pierre Batiffol</a> to the Novatian Bishop <a href="/wiki/Sisinnius_I_of_Constantinople" title="Sisinnius I of Constantinople">Sisinnius</a> (c. 400). The extant work under the title "On the Sovereignty of God" does not correspond with Eusebius' description of it, though <a href="/wiki/Adolf_von_Harnack" title="Adolf von Harnack">Adolf von Harnack</a> regarded it as still possibly Justin's, and at least of the 2nd century. The author of the smaller treatise <i>To the Greeks</i> cannot be Justin, because he is dependent on Tatian; Von Harnack placed it between 180 and 240.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Parisinus_graecus_450"><i>Parisinus graecus</i> 450</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Parisinus graecus 450">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>After this medieval period in which no authentic works of Justin Martyr were in widespread circulation, a single codex containing the complete works of Justin Martyr was discovered and purchased by <a href="/wiki/Guillaume_Pellicier" title="Guillaume Pellicier">Guillaume Pellicier</a>, around 1540 in Venice. Pellicier sent it to the <a href="/wiki/Biblioth%C3%A8que_nationale_de_France" title="Bibliothèque nationale de France">Bibliothèque nationale de France</a> where it remains today under the catalog number <i>Parisinus graecus</i> 450. This codex was completed on 11 September 1364 somewhere in the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Empire" title="Byzantine Empire">Byzantine Empire</a>. The name of the scribe is unknown, although <a href="/wiki/Manuel_Kantakouzenos" title="Manuel Kantakouzenos">Manuel Kantakouzenos</a> has been suggested as patron. Internal textual evidence shows that multiple older manuscripts were used to create this one, which strongly suggests that it must have originated in a major population center like <a href="/wiki/Mistra" class="mw-redirect" title="Mistra">Mistra</a>, since libraries holding Justin Martyr were already rare by 1364. Other partial medieval manuscripts have been shown to be copies of this one.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> The <i><a href="/wiki/Editio_princeps" title="Editio princeps">editio princeps</a></i> was published by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Estienne" title="Robert Estienne">Robert Estienne</a> in 1551.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Dialogue_with_Trypho">Dialogue with Trypho</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Dialogue with Trypho">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <i>Dialogue</i> is a later work than the <i><a href="/wiki/First_Apology_of_Justin_Martyr" title="First Apology of Justin Martyr">First Apology</a></i>; the date of composition of the latter, judging from the fact that it was addressed to <a href="/wiki/Antoninus_Pius" title="Antoninus Pius">Antoninus Pius</a> and his adopted sons <a href="/wiki/Marcus_Aurelius" title="Marcus Aurelius">Marcus Aurelius</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lucius_Verus" title="Lucius Verus">Lucius Verus</a>, must fall between 147 and 161. In the <i><a href="/wiki/Dialogue_with_Trypho" title="Dialogue with Trypho">Dialogue with Trypho</a></i>, after an introductory section, Justin undertakes to show that Christianity is the new law for all men.
</p><p>Justin's dialogue with Trypho is unique in that he provides information on tensions between Jewish and Gentile believers in Jesus of the second century (Dial. 47:2–3)<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> and in acknowledging the existence of a range, and a variety, of attitudes toward the beliefs and traditions of the Jewish believers in Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="On_the_Resurrection"><i>On the Resurrection</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: On the Resurrection">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"><table class="box-Unreferenced_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Unreferenced" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>does not <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources">cite</a> any <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">sources</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit">improve this section</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>. Unsourced material may be challenged and <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">removed</a>.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">December 2015</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>The treatise <i>On the Resurrection</i> exists in extensive fragments that are preserved in the <i>Sacra parallela</i>. The fragments begin with the assertion that the truth, and God the author of truth, need no witness, but that as a concession to the weakness of men it is necessary to give arguments to convince those who gainsay it. It is then shown, after a denial of unfounded deductions, that the resurrection of the body is neither impossible nor unworthy of God, and that the evidence of prophecy is not lacking for it. Another fragment takes up the positive proof of the resurrection, adducing that of Christ and of those whom he recalled to life. In yet another fragment the resurrection is shown to be that of what has gone down, i.e., the body; the knowledge concerning it is the new doctrine, in contrast to that of the old philosophers. The doctrine <a href="/wiki/Logical_consequence" title="Logical consequence">follows from</a> the command to keep the body in moral purity.
</p><p>The authenticity of the treatise is not so generally accepted as are Justin's other works.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Even so, earlier than the <i>Sacra parallela</i>, it is referred to by Procopius of Gaza (c. 465–528).  Methodius appeals to Justin in support of his interpretation of <a href="/wiki/First_Epistle_to_the_Corinthians" title="First Epistle to the Corinthians">1 Corinthians</a> 15:50 in a way that makes it natural to assume the existence of a treatise on the subject, to say nothing of other traces of a connection in thought both here in Irenaeus (V., ii.-xiii. 5) and in Tertullian, where it is too close to be anything but a conscious following of the Greek. The <i>Against Marcion</i> is lost, as is the <i>Refutation of all Heresies</i> to which Justin himself refers in <i>Apology</i>, i. 26; <a href="/wiki/Hegesippus_(chronicler)" title="Hegesippus (chronicler)">Hegesippus</a>, besides perhaps Irenaeus and Tertullian, seems to have used it.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Role_within_the_Church">Role within the Church</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Role within the Church">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1097763485"><table class="box-More_citations_needed_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a href="/wiki/Special:EditPage/Justin_Martyr" title="Special:EditPage/Justin Martyr">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>&#32;in this section. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">December 2015</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><a href="/wiki/Flacius" class="mw-redirect" title="Flacius">Flacius</a> discovered "blemishes" in Justin's theology, which he attributed to the influence of pagan philosophers; and in modern times <a href="/wiki/Johann_Salomo_Semler" title="Johann Salomo Semler">Semler</a> and S.G. Lange have made him out a thorough Hellene, while Semisch and Otto defend him from this charge.
</p><p>In opposition to the school of <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_Christian_Baur" title="Ferdinand Christian Baur">Ferdinand Christian Baur</a>, who considered him a Jewish Christian, <a href="/wiki/Albrecht_Ritschl" title="Albrecht Ritschl">Albrecht Ritschl</a> has argued that it was precisely because he was a Gentile Christian that he did not fully understand the <a href="/wiki/Old_Testament" title="Old Testament">Old Testament</a> foundation of Paul's teaching, and explained in this way the modified character of his Paulinism and his legal mode of thought.
</p><p>Engelhardt has attempted to extend this line of treatment to Justin's entire theology, and to show that his conceptions of God, of free will and righteousness, of redemption, grace, and merit prove the influence of the cultivated Greek pagan world of the 2nd century, dominated by the Platonic and Stoic philosophy. But he admits that Justin is a Christian in his unquestioning adherence to the Church and its faith, his unqualified recognition of the Old Testament, and his faith in Christ as the Son of God the Creator, made manifest in the flesh, crucified, and risen, through which belief he succeeds in getting away from the dualism of both pagan and Gnostic philosophy.
</p><p>Justin was confident that his teaching was that of the Church at large. He knows of a division among the orthodox only on the question of the <a href="/wiki/Millennialism" title="Millennialism">millennium</a> and on the attitude toward the milder Jewish Christianity, which he personally is willing to tolerate as long as its professors in their turn do not interfere with the liberty of the Gentile converts; his <a href="/wiki/Millenarianism" title="Millenarianism">millenarianism</a> seems to have no connection with <a href="/wiki/Judaism" title="Judaism">Judaism</a>, but he believes firmly in a millennium, and generally in the Christian <a href="/wiki/Eschatology" title="Eschatology">eschatology</a>.
</p><p>Opposition to Judaism was common among church leaders in his day; however, Justin Martyr was hostile towards Jewry and regarded Jews as an accursed people. His anti-Judaic polemics have been cited as an origin of Christian antisemitism.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> However his views elaborated in the <i><a href="/wiki/Dialogue_with_Trypho" title="Dialogue with Trypho">Dialogue with Trypho</a></i> were tame compared to those of <a href="/wiki/John_Chrysostom" title="John Chrysostom">John Chrysostom</a> and others.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Christology">Christology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Christology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Justin, like others, thought that the Greek philosophers had derived, if not borrowed, the most essential elements of truth found in their teaching from the <a href="/wiki/Old_Testament" title="Old Testament">Old Testament</a>. But at the same time he adopted the <a href="/wiki/Stoicism" title="Stoicism">Stoic</a> doctrine of the "seminal word," and so philosophy was to him an operation of the Word—in fact, through his identification of the Word with <a href="/wiki/Christ" class="mw-redirect" title="Christ">Christ</a>, it was brought into immediate connection with him.<sup id="cite_ref-NSH_284_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NSH_284-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thus he does not hesitate to declare that <a href="/wiki/Socrates" title="Socrates">Socrates</a> and <a href="/wiki/Heraclitus" title="Heraclitus">Heraclitus</a> were Christians (<i>Apol.</i>, i. 46, ii. 10). His aim was to emphasize the absolute significance of Christ, so that all that ever existed of virtue and truth may be referred to him. The old philosophers and law-givers had only a part of the <a href="/wiki/Logos" title="Logos">Logos</a>, while the whole appears in Christ.<sup id="cite_ref-NSH_284_47-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NSH_284-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While the gentile peoples, seduced by devils, had deserted the true God for idols, the Jews and Samaritans possessed the revelation given through the prophets and awaited the <a href="/wiki/Messiah" title="Messiah">Messiah</a>. However, the law, while containing commandments intended to promote the true fear of God, had other prescriptions of a purely pedagogic nature, which necessarily ceased when Christ, their end, appeared; of such temporary and merely relative regulations were <a href="/wiki/Circumcision" title="Circumcision">circumcision</a>, animal sacrifices, the <a href="/wiki/Shabbat" title="Shabbat">Sabbath</a>, and the laws as to food. Through Christ, the abiding law of God has been fully proclaimed.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> In his character, as the teacher of the new doctrine and promulgator of the new law, lies the essential nature of his redeeming work.<sup id="cite_ref-NSH_284_47-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NSH_284-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The idea of an economy of grace, of a restoration of the union with God which had been destroyed by sin, is not foreign to him. It is noteworthy that in the "Dialogue" he no longer speaks of a "seed of the Word" in every man, and in his non-apologetic works the emphasis is laid upon the redeeming acts of the life of Christ rather than upon the demonstration of the reasonableness and moral value of Christianity, though the fragmentary character of the latter works makes it difficult to determine exactly to what extent this is true and how far the teaching of <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a> on redemption is derived from him.<sup id="cite_ref-NSH_284_47-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NSH_284-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The 1913 <i><a href="/wiki/Catholic_Encyclopedia" title="Catholic Encyclopedia">Catholic Encyclopedia</a></i> notes that scholars have differed on whether Justin's writings on the nature of God were meant to express his firm opinion on points of doctrine, or to speculate on these matters.  Specific points Justin addressed include that the Logos is "numerically distinct from the Father" though "born of the very substance of the Father," and that "through the Word, God has made everything."  Justin used the metaphor of fire to describe the Logos as spreading like a flame, rather than "dividing" the substance of the Father.  He also defended the Holy Spirit as a member of the Trinity, as well as the virginal birth of Jesus Christ.  The Encyclopedia states that Justin places the genesis of the Logos as a voluntary act of the Father at the beginning of creation, noting that this is an "unfortunate" conflict with later Christian teachings.<sup id="cite_ref-OCE_martyr_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OCE_martyr-50">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Memoirs_of_the_apostles">Memoirs of the apostles</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Memoirs of the apostles">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Justin Martyr, in his <a href="/wiki/First_Apology_of_Justin_Martyr" title="First Apology of Justin Martyr"><i>First Apology</i></a> (c. 155) and <i><a href="/wiki/Dialogue_with_Trypho" title="Dialogue with Trypho">Dialogue with Trypho</a></i> (c. 160),<sup id="cite_ref-Rokeah_2002_p._2_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rokeah_2002_p._2-51">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> sometimes refers to written sources consisting of narratives of the life of <a href="/wiki/Jesus" title="Jesus">Jesus</a> and quotations of the <a href="/wiki/Sayings_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Sayings of Jesus">sayings of Jesus</a> as "memoirs of the <a href="/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament" title="Apostles in the New Testament">apostles</a>" (<a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: ἀπομνημονεύματα τῶν ἀποστόλων; <a href="/wiki/Transliteration" title="Transliteration">transliteration</a>: <i>apomnêmoneúmata tôn apostólôn</i>) and less frequently as <a href="/wiki/Gospel" title="Gospel">gospels</a> (Greek: εὐαγγέλιον; transliteration: <i>euangélion</i>) which, Justin says, were read every Sunday in the church at Rome (<i>1 Apol</i>. 67.3 – "and the memoirs of the apostles or the writings of the prophets are being read as long as it is allowable").<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._38_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_p._38-52">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The designation "memoirs of the apostles" occurs twice in Justin's <i>First Apology</i> (66.3, 67.3–4) and thirteen times in the <i>Dialogue</i>, mostly in his interpretation of Psalm 22, whereas the term "gospel" is used only three times, once in <i>1 Apol.</i> 66.3 and twice in the <i>Dialogue</i>.  The single passage where Justin uses both terms (<i>1 Apol.</i> 66.3) makes it clear that "memoirs of the apostles" and "gospels" are equivalent, and the use of the plural indicates Justin's awareness of more than one written gospel. ("The apostles in the memoirs which have come from them, which are also called gospels, have transmitted that the Lord had commanded...").<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._38,40–41_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._38,40–41-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>  Justin may have preferred the designation "memoirs of the apostles" as a contrast to the "gospel" of his contemporary <a href="/wiki/Marcion" class="mw-redirect" title="Marcion">Marcion</a> to emphasize the connections between the historical testimony of the gospels and the <a href="/wiki/Old_Testament" title="Old Testament">Old Testament</a> prophecies which Marcion rejected.<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._36–37,43_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._36–37,43-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The origin of Justin's use of the name "memoirs of the apostles" as a synonym for the gospels is uncertain. Scholar <a href="/wiki/David_E._Aune" class="mw-redirect" title="David E. Aune">David E. Aune</a> has argued that the gospels were modeled after classical <a href="/wiki/Greco-Roman" class="mw-redirect" title="Greco-Roman">Greco-Roman</a> biographies, and Justin's use of the term <i>apomnemoneumata</i> to mean all the <a href="/wiki/Synoptic_Gospels" title="Synoptic Gospels">Synoptic Gospels</a> should be understood as referring to a written biography such as the <i><a href="/wiki/Memorabilia_(Xenophon)" title="Memorabilia (Xenophon)">Memorabilia of Xenophon</a></i> because they preserve the authentic teachings of Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-Aune_1987_p._67_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Aune_1987_p._67-55">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> However, scholar <a href="/wiki/Helmut_Koester" title="Helmut Koester">Helmut Koester</a> has pointed out the Latin title "Memorabilia" was not applied to Xenophon's work until the Middle Ages, and it is more likely <i>apomnemoneumata</i> was used to describe the oral transmission of the sayings of Jesus in <a href="/wiki/Early_Christianity" title="Early Christianity">early Christianity</a>. <a href="/wiki/Papias_of_Hierapolis" title="Papias of Hierapolis">Papias</a> uses a similar term meaning "remembered" (<i>apomnemoneusen</i>) when describing how <a href="/wiki/St._Mark" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Mark">Mark</a> accurately recorded the "recollections of <a href="/wiki/St._Peter" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Peter">Peter</a>", and Justin also uses it in reference to Peter in <i>Dial.</i> 106.3, followed by a quotation found only in the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Mark" title="Gospel of Mark">Gospel of Mark</a> (Mk 3:16–17).  Therefore, according to Koester, it is likely that Justin applied the name "memoirs of the apostles" analogously to indicate the trustworthy recollections of the apostles found in the written record of the gospels.<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._33–34,38–40_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._33–34,38–40-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Justin expounded on the gospel texts as an accurate recording of the <a href="/wiki/Bible_prophecy" title="Bible prophecy">fulfillment of prophecy</a>, which he combined with quotations of the <a href="/wiki/Nevi%27im" title="Nevi&#39;im">prophets of Israel</a> from the <a href="/wiki/LXX" class="mw-redirect" title="LXX">LXX</a> to demonstrate a proof from prophecy of the <a href="/wiki/Kerygma" title="Kerygma">Christian kerygma</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._377_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_p._377-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>  The importance which Justin attaches to the words of the prophets, which he regularly quotes with the formula "it is written", shows his estimate of the Old Testament Scriptures.  However, the scriptural authority he attributes to the "memoirs of the apostles" is less certain.  Koester articulates a majority view among scholars that Justin considered the "memoirs of the apostles" to be accurate historical records but not inspired writings,<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._41_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_p._41-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> whereas scholar <a href="/wiki/Charles_E._Hill" title="Charles E. Hill">Charles E. Hill</a>, though acknowledging the position of mainstream scholarship, contends that Justin regarded the fulfillment quotations of the gospels to be equal in authority.<sup id="cite_ref-Hill_2004_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hill_2004-59">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Composition">Composition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Composition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Scriptural_sources">Scriptural sources</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Scriptural sources">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Gospels">Gospels</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Gospels">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Justin uses material from the Synoptic Gospels (Matthew, Mark, and Luke) in the composition of the <i>First Apology</i> and the <i>Dialogue</i>, either directly, as in the case of Matthew,<sup id="cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._130,163_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_pp._130,163-60">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> or indirectly through the use of a <a href="/wiki/Gospel_harmony" title="Gospel harmony">gospel harmony</a>, which may have been composed by Justin or his school.<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_2000_p._344_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_2000_p._344-61">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>  However, his use, or even knowledge, of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_John" title="Gospel of John">Gospel of John</a> is uncertain.  One possible reference to John is a saying that is quoted in the context of a description of Christian baptism (<i>1 Apol</i>. 61.4 – "Unless you are reborn, you cannot enter into the kingdom of heaven.").  However, Koester contends that Justin obtained this saying from a baptismal liturgy rather than a written gospel.<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._360–361_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._360–361-62">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Justin's possible knowledge of John's gospel may be suggested by verbal similarities to John 3:4 directly after the discussion about the new birth ("Now, that it is impossible for those who have once been born to enter their mother's womb is manifest to all"). Justin also uses language very similar to that of John 1:20 and 1:28. Furthermore, by employing the term "memoirs of the apostles" and distinguishing them from the writings of their "followers", Justin must have been of the belief that at least two gospels were written by actual apostles.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Apocalypse">Apocalypse</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Apocalypse">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Justin does not quote from the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Revelation" title="Book of Revelation">Book of Revelation</a> directly, yet he clearly refers to it, naming <a href="/wiki/John_of_Patmos" title="John of Patmos">John</a> as its author (<i>Dial</i>. 81.4 "Moreover also among us a man named John, one of the apostles of Christ, prophesied in a revelation made to him that those who have believed on our Christ will spend a thousand years in Jerusalem; and that hereafter the general and, in short, the eternal resurrection and judgment of all will likewise take place").  Scholar <a href="/wiki/Brooke_Foss_Westcott" title="Brooke Foss Westcott">Brooke Foss Westcott</a> notes that this reference to the author of the single prophetic book of the New Testament illustrates the distinction Justin made between the role of prophecy and fulfillment quotations from the gospels, as Justin does not mention any of the individual <a href="/wiki/Canonical_gospels" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonical gospels">canonical gospels</a> by name.<sup id="cite_ref-Westcott_1875_p._120_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Westcott_1875_p._120-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Letters">Letters</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Letters">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The apologetic character of Justin's habit of thought appears again in the Acts of his martyrdom, the genuineness of which is attested by internal evidence.<sup id="cite_ref-Bonwetsch_1914_p._284_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bonwetsch_1914_p._284-64">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Testimony_sources">Testimony sources</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Testimony sources">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>According to scholar <a href="/wiki/Oskar_Skarsaune" title="Oskar Skarsaune">Oskar Skarsaune</a>, Justin relies on two main sources for his proofs from prophecy that probably circulated as collections of scriptural testimonies within his Christian school.  He refers to Justin's primary source for demonstrating scriptural proofs in the <i>First Apology</i> and parallel passages in the <i>Dialogue</i> as a "kerygma source".  A second source, which was used only in the <i>Dialogue</i>, may be identical to a lost dialogue attributed to <a href="/wiki/Aristo_of_Pella" title="Aristo of Pella">Aristo of Pella</a> on the divine nature of the <a href="/wiki/Messiah#Christianity" title="Messiah">Messiah</a>, the <i><a href="/wiki/Dialogue_of_Jason_and_Papiscus" title="Dialogue of Jason and Papiscus">Dialogue of Jason and Papiscus</a></i> (c. 140).  Justin brings in biblical quotes verbatim from these sources, and he often appears to be paraphrasing his sources very closely, even in his interpretive remarks.<sup id="cite_ref-Skarsaune_2007_pp._380–381_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skarsaune_2007_pp._380–381-65">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Justin occasionally uses the Gospel of Matthew directly as a source for Old Testament prophecies to supplement his testimony sources.<sup id="cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._130,163_60-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_pp._130,163-60">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._382–383_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._382–383-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>  However, the fulfillment quotations from these sources most often appear to be harmonizations of the gospels of Matthew and Luke.<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._365_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_p._365-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>  Koester suggests that Justin had composed an early harmony along the lines of his pupil <a href="/wiki/Tatian" title="Tatian">Tatian</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Diatesseron" class="mw-redirect" title="Diatesseron">Diatesseron</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_2000_p._344_61-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_2000_p._344-61">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>  However, the existence of a harmony independent of a collection of sayings for exposition purposes has been disputed by scholar <a href="/w/index.php?title=Arthur_Bellinzoni&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Arthur Bellinzoni (page does not exist)">Arthur Bellinzoni</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Bellinzoni_1967_p._141_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bellinzoni_1967_p._141-68">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._370_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_p._370-69">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> The question of whether the harmonized gospel materials found in Justin's writings came from a preexisting gospel harmony or were assembled as part of an integral process of creating scriptural <a href="/wiki/Prooftext" title="Prooftext">prooftexts</a> is an ongoing subject of scholarly investigation.<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._378_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_p._378-70">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id=".22Kerygma_source.22"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="&quot;Kerygma_source&quot;">"Kerygma source"</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: &quot;Kerygma source&quot;">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The following excerpt from <i>1 Apol</i>. 33:1,4–5 (partial parallel in <i>Dial</i>. 84) on the <a href="/wiki/Annunciation" title="Annunciation">annunciation</a> and <a href="/wiki/Virgin_birth_of_Jesus" title="Virgin birth of Jesus">virgin birth of Jesus</a> shows how Justin used harmonized gospel verses from Matthew and Luke to provide a scriptural proof of the messiahship of Jesus based on fulfillment of the prophecy of <a href="/wiki/Isaiah_7:14" title="Isaiah 7:14">Isaiah 7:14</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_p._145_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_p._145-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>And hear again how Isaiah in express words foretold that He should be born of a virgin; for he spoke thus: 'Behold, the virgin will conceive in the womb and bear a son, and they will say in his name, God with us' (Mt 1:23).</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><i>1 Apol</i>. 33:1<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._379_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_p._379-72">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._32–34_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_pp._32–34-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>...the power of God, coming down upon <a href="/wiki/Mary_(mother_of_Jesus)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary (mother of Jesus)">the virgin</a>, overshadowed her and made her while yet a virgin to conceive (cf. Lk 1:35), and the angel of God proclaimed to her and said, 'Behold, you will conceive in the womb from the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Spirit" title="Holy Spirit">Holy Spirit</a> and bear a son (Mt 1:20/Lk 1:31) and he will be called Son of the Most High (Lk 1:32). And you shall call his name Jesus, because he will save his people from their sins (Mt 1:21),' as <u>those who have made memoirs</u> of all things about our savior Jesus Christ taught...</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><i>1 Apol</i>. 33:4–5<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._380–81_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._380–81-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>According to Skarsaune, the harmonized gospel narratives of Matthew and Luke were part of a tradition already circulating within Justin's school that expounded on the life and work of Jesus as the Messiah and the apostolic mission.  Justin then rearranged and expanded these testimonia to create his <i>First Apology</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._143,425_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_pp._143,425-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Skarsaune_2007_pp._381–85_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skarsaune_2007_pp._381–85-76">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>  The "kerygma source" of <a href="/wiki/Prooftext" title="Prooftext">prooftexts</a> (contained within <i>1 Apol</i>. 31–53) is believed to have had a <a href="/wiki/Second_Coming" title="Second Coming">Two Parousias</a> Christology, characterized by the belief that Jesus first came in humility, in fulfillment of prophecy, and will <a href="/wiki/Son_of_man#Daniel" title="Son of man">return in glory</a> as the <a href="/wiki/Supersessionism" title="Supersessionism">Messiah to the Gentiles</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._154–56_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_pp._154–56-77">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>  There are close literary parallels between the Christology of Justin's source and the <i><a href="/wiki/Apocalypse_of_Peter" title="Apocalypse of Peter">Apocalypse of Peter</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Skarsaune_2007_pp._388–9_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skarsaune_2007_pp._388–9-78">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Dialogue_of_Jason_and_Papiscus"><i>Dialogue of Jason and Papiscus</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Dialogue of Jason and Papiscus">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The following excerpts from the <i>Dialogue with Trypho</i> of the <a href="/wiki/Baptism_of_Jesus" title="Baptism of Jesus">baptism</a> (<i>Dial</i>. 88:3,8) and <a href="/wiki/Temptation_of_Christ" title="Temptation of Christ">temptation</a> (<i>Dial</i>. 103:5–6) of Jesus, which are believed to have originated from the <i>Dialogue of Jason and Papiscus</i>, illustrate the use of gospel narratives and sayings of Jesus in a testimony source and how Justin has adopted these "memoirs of the apostles" for his own purposes.
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>And then, when Jesus had come to the <a href="/wiki/Jordan_River" title="Jordan River">river Jordan</a> where <a href="/wiki/John_the_Baptist" title="John the Baptist">John</a> was <a href="/wiki/Baptism" title="Baptism">baptizing</a>, and when Jesus came down into the water, a fire was even kindled in the Jordan, and when He was rising up from the water, the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Spirit" title="Holy Spirit">Holy Spirit</a> fluttered down upon Him in the form of a dove, as the <u>apostles have written</u> about this very <a href="/wiki/Christ" class="mw-redirect" title="Christ">Christ</a> of ours.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><i>Dial</i>. 88:3</cite></div></blockquote>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>And when Jesus came to the Jordan, and being supposed to be the son of <a href="/wiki/St._Joseph" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Joseph">Joseph</a> the carpenter..., the Holy Spirit, and for man's sake, as I said before, fluttered down upon Him, and a voice came at the time out of the heavens – which was spoken also by <a href="/wiki/David" title="David">David</a>, when he said, impersonating Christ, what the <a href="/wiki/God_the_Father" title="God the Father">Father</a> was going to say to Him – 'You are <a href="/wiki/Son_of_God" title="Son of God">My Son</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke#Disputed_verses" title="Gospel of Luke">this day I have begotten you</a>'."</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><i>Dial</i>. 88:8<sup id="cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_p._198_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_p._198-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>...the <a href="/wiki/Devil" title="Devil">Devil</a> himself,...[was] called <a href="/wiki/Serpent_(Bible)" class="mw-redirect" title="Serpent (Bible)">serpent</a> by <a href="/wiki/Moses" title="Moses">Moses</a>, the Devil by <a href="/wiki/Job_(Biblical_figure)" class="mw-redirect" title="Job (Biblical figure)">Job</a> and <a href="/wiki/Zechariah_(Hebrew_prophet)" title="Zechariah (Hebrew prophet)">Zachariah</a>, and was addressed as <a href="/wiki/Satan" title="Satan">Satanas</a> by Jesus.  This indicated that he had a compound name made up of the actions which he performed; for the word "Sata" in the <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a> and <a href="/wiki/Aramaic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Aramaic language">Syrian</a> tongue means "<a href="/wiki/Apostate#Christianity" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostate">apostate</a>", while "<a href="/wiki/Serpent_(Bible)" class="mw-redirect" title="Serpent (Bible)">nas</a>" is the word which means in translation "serpent", thus, from both parts is formed the one word "Sata-nas".  It is narrated in the <u>memoirs of the apostles</u> that as soon as Jesus came up out of the river Jordan and a voice said to him: 'You are My Son, this day I have begotten you', this Devil came and tempted him, even so far as to exclaim: 'Worship me'; but Christ replied: 'Get behind me, Satanas, the Lord your God shall you worship, and Him only shall you serve'.  For, since the Devil had deceived <a href="/wiki/Adam" title="Adam">Adam</a>, he fancied that he could in some way harm him also.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite><i>Dial</i>. 103:5–6<sup id="cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._222–23,238,383–84,393_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_pp._222–23,238,383–84,393-80">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>The quotations refer to the fulfillment of a prophecy of <a href="/wiki/Psalm_2" title="Psalm 2">Psalm 2:7</a> found in the <a href="/wiki/Western_text-type" title="Western text-type">Western text-type</a> of Luke 3:22.<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._394–395_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._394–395-81">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Justin's mention of the fire on the Jordan without comment suggests that he was relying on an intermediate source for these gospel quotations,<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._395_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_p._395-82">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> and his literal interpretation of a pseudo-etymology of the Hebrew word Satan indicates a dependence on a testimony source with a knowledge of Hebrew, which was probably the <i>Dialogue of Jason and Papiscus</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Rokeah_2002_pp._20–21_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rokeah_2002_pp._20–21-83">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <i>Dialogue</i> attributed to Aristo of Pella is believed to have furnished Justin with scriptural <a href="/wiki/Prooftext" title="Prooftext">prooftexts</a> on the divinity of the Messiah by combining a <a href="/wiki/Wisdom" title="Wisdom">Wisdom</a> Christology – Christ as the incarnation of preexistent Wisdom – with a <a href="/wiki/Second_Adam" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Adam">Second Adam</a> Christology – the first Adam was conquered by Satan, but this <a href="/wiki/Fall_of_Man" class="mw-redirect" title="Fall of Man">Fall of Man</a> is reversed by Christ as the Second Adam who conquers Satan.  This is implied in the pseudo-etymology in <i>Dial</i>. 103:5–6 linking the name of Satan to the "apostate-serpent".  The Christology of the source is close to that of the <i><a href="/wiki/Ascension_of_Isaiah" title="Ascension of Isaiah">Ascension of Isaiah</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Skarsaune_2007_pp._399–400_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skarsaune_2007_pp._399–400-84">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Catechetical_sources">Catechetical sources</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Catechetical sources">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Justin quotes many sayings of Jesus in <i>1 Apol</i>. 15–17 and smaller sayings clusters in <i>Dial</i>. 17:3–4; 35:3; 51:2–3; and 76:4–7.  The sayings are most often harmonizations of Matthew and Luke that appear to be grouped together topically and organized into sayings collections, including material that probably originated from an early Christian <a href="/wiki/Catechism" title="Catechism">catechism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._361_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_p._361-85">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._99–100_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._99–100-86">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The following example of an ethical teaching <a href="/wiki/Expounding_of_the_Law#Oaths" class="mw-redirect" title="Expounding of the Law">On Swearing Oaths</a> in <i>1 Apol</i>. 16:5 shows a combination of sayings material found in Matthew and the <a href="/wiki/Epistle_of_James" title="Epistle of James">Epistle of James</a>:
</p>
<blockquote><p>Do not swear at all (Mt 5:34).  Let your Yes be Yes and your No be No (Jas 5:12).  Everything beyond these is from evil (Mt 5:37).</p></blockquote>
<p>The saying "Let your Yes be Yes and your No be No" from James 5:12 is interpolated into a sayings complex from Matthew 5:34,37.  The text appears in a large number of <a href="/wiki/Patristic" class="mw-redirect" title="Patristic">Patristic</a> quotations and twice in the <a href="/wiki/Clementine_literature" title="Clementine literature">Clementine Homilies</a> (<i>Hom</i>. 3:55, 19:2).  Thus, it is likely that Justin was quoting this harmonized text from a catechism.<sup id="cite_ref-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._64–67_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._64–67-87">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._363_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_p._363-88">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The harmonization of Matthew and Luke is evident in the following quotations of Mt 7:22–23 and Lk 13:26–27, which are used by Justin twice, in <i>1 Apol</i>. 16:11 and <i>Dial</i>. 76:5:
</p>
<blockquote><p>Many will say to me, 'Lord, Lord, did we not in your name eat and drink and do powerful deeds?' And then I shall say to them, 'go away from me, workers of lawlessness'.</p></blockquote>
<blockquote><p>Many will say to me on that day, 'Lord, Lord, did we not in your name eat and drink and prophecy and drive out demons?' And I shall say to them, 'go away from me'.</p></blockquote>
<p>In both cases, Justin is using the same harmonized text of Matthew and Luke, although neither of the quotations includes the entire text of those gospel passages.  The last phrase, "workers of lawlessness", has an exact parallel with <a href="/wiki/2_Clement" class="mw-redirect" title="2 Clement">2 Clement</a> 4:5.  This harmonized text also appears in a large number of quotations by the <a href="/wiki/Church_Fathers" title="Church Fathers">Church Fathers</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._356,365–67_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._356,365–67-89">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._22–25_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._22–25-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>  <i>1 Apol</i>. 16:11 is part of a larger unit of sayings material in <i>1 Apol</i> 16:9–13 which combines a warning against being unprepared with a warning against false prophets.  The entire unit is a carefully composed harmony of parallel texts from Matthew and Luke.<sup id="cite_ref-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._98–99_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._98–99-91">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._367–370_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._367–370-92">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>  This unit is part of a larger collection of sayings found in <i>1 Apol</i>. 15–17 that appear to have originated from a catechism used by Justin's school in Rome, which may have had a wide circulation.  Justin excerpted and rearranged the catechetical sayings material to create <i>Apol</i>. 15–17 and parallel passages in the <i>Dialogue</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Bellinzoni_1967_p._100_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bellinzoni_1967_p._100-93">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._375_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Koester_1990_p._375-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_sources">Other sources</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Other sources">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Justin includes a tract on Greek mythology in <i>1 Apol</i>. 54 and <i>Dial</i>. 69 which asserts that myths about various pagan deities are imitations of the prophecies about Christ in the Old Testament.  There is also a small tract in <i>1 Apol</i>. 59–60 on borrowings of the philosophers from Moses, particularly Plato.  These two tracts may be from the same source, which may have been an early Christian <i>Apology</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._52–53,148–150,431_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_pp._52–53,148–150,431-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Prophetic_exegesis">Prophetic exegesis</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Prophetic exegesis">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Justin's writings constitute a storehouse of early interpretation of the prophetic Scriptures.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Belief_in_prophecy">Belief in prophecy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Belief in prophecy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The truth of the prophets, he declares, compels assent. He considered the Old Testament an inspired guide and counselor. He was converted by a Christian philosopher whom he paraphrased as saying:
</p>
<dl><dd>"There existed, long before this time, certain men more ancient than all those who are esteemed philosophers, both righteous and beloved by God, who spoke by the Divine Spirit, and foretold events which would take place, and which are now taking place. They are called prophets. These alone both saw and announced the truth to men, neither reverencing nor fearing any man. not influenced by a desire for glory, but speaking those things alone which they saw and which they heard, being filled with the Holy Spirit. Their writings are still extant, and he who has read them is very much helped in his knowledge of the beginning and end of things. . . And those events which have happened, and those which are happening, compel you to assent to the utterances made by them."<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup></dd></dl>
<p>Then Justin told his own experience:
</p>
