<dl><dd>"Straightway a flame was kindled in my soul; and a love of the prophets, and of those men who are friends of Christ, possessed me; and whilst revolving his words in my mind, I found this philosophy alone to be safe and profitable."<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup></dd></dl>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Fulfillment">Fulfillment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Fulfillment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Justin listed the following events as fulfillments of Bible prophecy:
</p>
<ul><li>The prophecies concerning the Messiah, and the particulars of His life.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The destruction of Jerusalem.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The Gentiles accepting Christianity.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Isaiah predicted that Jesus would be born of a virgin.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Micah mentions Bethlehem as the place of His birth.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Zechariah forecasts His entry into Jerusalem on the foal of an ass (a donkey).<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_Advent_and_Daniel_7">Second Advent and Daniel 7</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Second Advent and Daniel 7">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Justin connected the Second Advent with the climax of the prophecy of Daniel 7.
</p>
<dl><dd>"But if so great a power is shown to have followed and to be still following the dispensation of His suffering, how great shall that be which shall follow His glorious advent!  For He shall come on the clouds as the Son of man, so Daniel foretold, and His angels shall come with Him. [Then follows Dan. 7:9–28.]"<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup></dd></dl>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Antichrist">Antichrist</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Antichrist">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The second advent Justin placed close upon the heels of the appearance of the "man of apostasy", i.e., the <a href="/wiki/Antichrist" title="Antichrist">Antichrist</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Time.2C_times.2C_and_a_half"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Time,_times,_and_a_half">Time, times, and a half</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Time, times, and a half">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Daniel's "<a href="/wiki/Time,_times,_and_half_a_time" class="mw-redirect" title="Time, times, and half a time">time, times, and a half</a>", Justin believed, was nearing its consummation, when the Antichrist would speak his blasphemies against the Most High.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Eucharist">Eucharist</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Eucharist">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Justin's statements are some of the earliest Christian expressions on the Eucharist.
</p>
<dl><dd>"And this food is called among us Εὐχαριστία [the Eucharist] ... For not as common bread and common drink do we receive these; but in like manner as Jesus Christ our Saviour, having been made flesh by the Word of God, had both flesh and blood for our salvation, so likewise have we been taught that the food which is blessed by the prayer of His word, and from which our blood and flesh by transmutation are nourished, is the flesh and blood of that Jesus who was made flesh."<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup></dd></dl>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Editions">Editions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Editions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p><b>Greek texts</b>:
</p>
<ul><li><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.bricecjones.com/blog/new-discovery-the-earliest-manuscript-of-justin-martyr-poxy-5129">P.Oxy.5129</a> (Egyptian Exploration Society, 4th century)</li>
<li>Thirlby, S., London, 1722.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Prudentius_Maran" title="Prudentius Maran">Maran, P.</a>, Paris, 1742 (the Benedictine edition, reprinted in <a href="/wiki/Migne" class="mw-redirect" title="Migne">Migne</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Patrologia_Graeca" title="Patrologia Graeca">Patrologia Graeca</a></i>, Vol. VI. Paris, 1857).</li>
<li>Otto, J. C., Jena, 1842 (3d ed., 1876–1881).</li>
<li>Krüger, G., Leipzig, 1896 (3d ed., Tübingen, 1915).</li>
<li>In <i>Die ältesten Apologeten</i>, ed. G.J. Goodspeed, (Göttingen, 1914; reprint 1984).<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i>Iustini Martyris Dialogus cum Tryphone</i>, ed <a href="/wiki/Miroslav_Marcovich" title="Miroslav Marcovich">Miroslav Marcovich</a> (Patristische Texte und Studien 47, Berlin/New York: de Gruyter, 1997).</li>
<li>Minns, Denis, and <a href="/wiki/Paul_Parvis" title="Paul Parvis">Paul Parvis</a>. <i>Justin, Philosopher and Martyr: Apologies</i>. Edited by Henry Chadwick, Oxford Early Christian Texts. Oxford: OUP, 2009. (In addition to translating into English has a critical Greek text).</li>
<li>Philippe Bobichon (ed.), <i>Justin Martyr, Dialogue avec Tryphon</i>, édition critique, introduction, texte grec, traduction, commentaires, appendices, indices, (Coll. Paradosis nos. 47, vol. I-II.) Editions Universitaires de Fribourg Suisse, (1125 pp.), 2003 <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.academia.edu/7280008/JUSTIN_MARTYR_Dialogue_avec_Tryphon_Dialogue_with_Trypho_%C3%A9dition_critique_VOLUME_I_Introduction_Texte_grec_Traduction_Coll_Paradosis_%C3%A9ditions_universitaires_de_Fribourg_Suisse_no_47_1_2003_563_pages">online</a></li></ul>
<p><b>English translations:</b>
</p>
<ul><li>Halton, TP and M Slusser, eds, <i>Dialogue with Trypho</i>, trans TB Falls, Selections from the Fathers of the Church, 3, (Washington, DC: Catholic University of America Press)</li>
<li>Minns, Denis, &amp; Paul Parvis. <i>Justin, Philosopher and Martyr: Apologies</i>. Edited by Henry Chadwick, Oxford Early Christian Texts. Oxford: OUP, 2009.</li></ul>
<p><b>Georgian translation:</b>
</p>
<ul><li>"Sulieri Venakhi", I, The First and Second Apology of Saint Justin Philosopher and Martyr, translated from Old Greek into Georgian, submitted with preface and comments by a monk Ekvtime Krupitski, Tbilisi Theological Academy, Tsalka, Sameba village, Cross Monastery, "Sulieri venakhi" Publishers, Tbilisi, 2022, ISBN 978-9941-9676-1-0</li>
<li>"Sulieri Venakhi", II, Saint Justin Martyr's dialogue with Trypho the Jew, translated from Old Greek into Georgian, submitted with preface and comments by a monk Ekvtime Krupitski, Tbilisi Theological Academy, Tsalka, Sameba village, Cross Monastery, "Sulieri venakhi" Publishers, Tbilisi, 2019, ISBN 978-9941-8-1570-6</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Literary_references">Literary references</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Literary references">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><i>The Rector of Justin</i> (1964), perhaps <a href="/wiki/Louis_Auchincloss" title="Louis Auchincloss">Louis Auchincloss</a>'s best-regarded novel, is the tale of a renowned <a href="/wiki/Headmaster" class="mw-redirect" title="Headmaster">headmaster</a> of a <a href="/wiki/New_England" title="New England">New England</a> <a href="/wiki/University-preparatory_school" class="mw-redirect" title="University-preparatory school">prep school</a>—similar to <a href="/wiki/Groton_School" title="Groton School">Groton</a>—and how he came to found his institution. He chooses the name Justin Martyr for his <a href="/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_seminaries_affiliated_with_the_Episcopal_Church" title="List of colleges and seminaries affiliated with the Episcopal Church">Episcopal school</a>. ("The school was named for the early  martyr and scholar who tried to reconcile the thinking of the Greek philosophers with the doctrines of Christ. Not for Prescott [the headmaster] were the humble fishermen who had their faith and faith alone."<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>)</li></ul>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Justin_Martyr&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

</li>

</li>

Oxford: Oxford University Press. pp.&#160;3–13. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0199542505" title="Special:BookSources/978-0199542505"><bdi>978-0199542505</bdi></a>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Justin%2C+philosopher+and+martyr+%3A+Apologies&amp;rft.place=Oxford&amp;rft.pages=3-13&amp;rft.pub=Oxford+University+Press&amp;rft.date=2009&amp;rft.isbn=978-0199542505&amp;rft.aulast=Justin&amp;rft.aufirst=Martyr%2C+Saint&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AJustin+Martyr" class="Z3988"></span><span class="cs1-maint citation-comment"><code class="cs1-code">{{<a href="/wiki/Template:Cite_book" title="Template:Cite book">cite book</a>}}</code>:  CS1 maint: multiple names: authors list (<a href="/wiki/Category:CS1_maint:_multiple_names:_authors_list" title="Category:CS1 maint: multiple names: authors list">link</a>)</span></span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-44"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-44">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Philippe Bobichon, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.academia.edu/7279808/_Justin_Martyr_%C3%A9tude_stylistique_du_Dialogue_avec_Tryphon_suivie_d_une_comparaison_avec_l_Apologie_et_le_De_resurrectione_Recherches_augustiniennes_et_patristiques_34_2005_pp_1_61">"Justin Martyr&#160;: étude stylistique du Dialogue avec Tryphon suivie d’une comparaison avec l’Apologie et le De resurrectione"</a>, <i>Recherches augustiniennes et patristiques</i> 34 (2005), pp. 1-61.</span>

<li id="cite_note-45"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-45">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation web cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.jewishvirtuallibrary.org/jsource/anti-semitism/Early_Church.html">"The Early Church and the Beginnings of Anti-Semitism"</a>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.btitle=The+Early+Church+and+the+Beginnings+of+Anti-Semitism&amp;rft_id=http%3A%2F%2Fwww.jewishvirtuallibrary.org%2Fjsource%2Fanti-semitism%2FEarly_Church.html&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AJustin+Martyr" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-46"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-46">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Philippe Bobichon, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.academia.edu/7280008/JUSTIN_MARTYR_Dialogue_avec_Tryphon_Dialogue_with_Trypho_%C3%A9dition_critique_VOLUME_I_Introduction_Texte_grec_Traduction_Coll_Paradosis_%C3%A9ditions_universitaires_de_Fribourg_Suisse_no_47_1_2003_563_pages"><i>Dialogue avec Tryphon, édition critique. <li id="cite_note-NSH_284-47"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-NSH_284_47-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-NSH_284_47-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-NSH_284_47-2"><sup><i><b>c</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-NSH_284_47-3"><sup><i><b>d</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text">New Schaff-Herzog Encyclopedia of Religious Knowledge 3rd ed. 1914. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.ccel.org/ccel/schaff/encyc06/Page_284.html">Pg 284</a>[Exact Quote]</span>

<li id="cite_note-48"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-48">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Philippe Bobichon, <i>Filiation divine du Christ et filiation divine des chrétiens dans les écrits de Justin Martyr</i> in P. de Navascués Benlloch, M. Crespo Losada and A. Sáez Gutiérrez (dir.), <i>Filiación. Cultura pagana, religión de Israel, orígenes del cristianismo</i>, vol. </li>
<li id="cite_note-49"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-49">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Philippe Bobichon, "Préceptes éternels et Loi mosaïque dans le Dialogue avec Tryphon de Justin Martyr", <i>Revue Biblique</i> 3/2 (2004), pp. 238-254 <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.academia.edu/7279745/_Pr%C3%A9ceptes_%C3%A9ternels_et_Loi_mosa%C3%AFque_dans_le_Dialogue_avec_Tryphon_de_Justin_Martyr_Revue_Biblique_3_2_2004_p_238_254">online</a> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-OCE_martyr-50"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-OCE_martyr_50-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/62/PD-icon.svg/12px-PD-icon.svg.png" decoding="async" width="12" height="12" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/62/PD-icon.svg/18px-PD-icon.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/62/PD-icon.svg/24px-PD-icon.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="196" data-file-height="196" /></span></span>&#160;One or more of the preceding sentences incorporates text from a publication now in the <a href="/wiki/Public_domain" title="Public domain">public domain</a>:&#160;<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFLebreton1910" class="citation encyclopaedia cs1">Lebreton, Jules (1910). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.newadvent.org/cathen/08580c.htm">"St. Justin Martyr"</a>. In Herbermann, Charles (ed.). <i><a href="/wiki/Catholic_Encyclopedia" title="Catholic Encyclopedia">Catholic Encyclopedia</a></i>. Vol.&#160;7. New York: Robert Appleton Company<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">2 November</span> 2013</span>.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=St.+Justin+Martyr&amp;rft.btitle=Catholic+Encyclopedia&amp;rft.place=New+York&amp;rft.pub=Robert+Appleton+Company&amp;rft.date=1910&amp;rft.aulast=Lebreton&amp;rft.aufirst=Jules&amp;rft_id=http%3A%2F%2Fwww.newadvent.org%2Fcathen%2F08580c.htm&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AJustin+Martyr" class="Z3988"></span> <p> The Word is numerically distinct from the Father (Dial., cxxviii, cxxix; cf. lvi, lxii). He was born of the very substance of the Father, not that this substance was divided, but He proceeds from it as one fire does from another at which it is lit (cxxviii, lxi); this form of production (procession) is compared also with that of human speech (lxi). The Word (Logos) is therefore the Son: much more, He alone may properly be called Son (II Apol., vi, 3); He is the monogenes, the unigenitus (Dial., cv). Elsewhere, however, Justin, like St. Paul, calls Him the eldest Son, prototokos (I Apol., xxxiii; xlvi; lxiii; Dial., lxxxiv, lxxxv, cxxv). The Word is God (I Apol., lxiii; Dial., xxxiv, xxxvi, xxxvii, lvi, lxiii, lxxvi, lxxxvi, lxxxvii, cxiii, cxv, cxxv, cxxvi, cxviii). His Divinity, however, seems subordinate, as does the worship which is rendered to Him (I Apol., vi; cf. lxi, 13; Teder, "Justins des Märtyrers Lehre von Jesus Christus", Freiburg im Br., 1906, 103–19). The Father engendered Him by a free and voluntary act (Dial., lxi, c, cxxvii, cxxviii; cf. Teder, op. cit., 104), at the beginning of all His works (Dial., lxi, lxii, II Apol., vi, 3); in this last text certain authors thought they distinguished in the Word two states of being, one intimate, the other outspoken, but this distinction, though found in some other apologists, is in Justin very doubtful. Through the Word God has made everything (II Apol., vi; Dial., cxiv). The Word is diffused through all humanity (I Apol., vi; II, viii; xiii); it was He who appeared to the patriarchs (I Apol., lxii; lxiii; Dial., lvi, lix, lx etc.). Two influences are plainly discernible in the aforesaid body of doctrine. It is, of course, to Christian revelation that Justin owes his concept of the distinct personality of the Word, His Divinity and Incarnation; but philosophic speculation is responsible for his unfortunate concepts of the temporal and voluntary generation of the Word, and for the subordinationism of Justin's theology. It must be recognized, moreover, that the latter ideas stand out more boldly in the "Apology" than in the "Dialogue."
</p></span></li>
<li id="cite_note-Rokeah_2002_p._2-51"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Rokeah_2002_p._2_51-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Rokeah (2002) <i>Justin Martyr and the Jews</i> p.&#160;2 – His <i>First Apology</i> dates from about 155 CE, for it mentions (chap. 29) the procurator of Egypt, Felix, who served in this capacity between 151 and 154.  Grant (<i>Greek Apologists</i> pp.&#160;53–54) links the <i>First Apology</i> to the martyrdom of Polycarp, the bishop of <a href="/wiki/Smyrna" title="Smyrna">Smyrna</a>, which occurred in 155 or 156; he finds allusions in the <i>Apology</i> to the description of Polycarp's death at the stake found in a letter sent by the Christian community of Smyrna to other Christian communities immediately after the event.&#160;... The <i>First Apology</i> is mentioned in the <i>Dialogue</i> (end of chap. 120), and it is therefore likely that the latter was composed around 160 CE."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_p._38-52"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._38_52-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancient Christian Gospels</i> p.&#160;38 – "It is clear that these "memoirs" are indeed gospel writings and that they are used liturgically as instructions for the sacrament and as texts for homilies."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._38,40–41-53"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._38,40–41_53-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancient Christian Gospels: Their History and Development</i> pp.&#160;38,40–41; p.&#160;38 – <i>Dial.</i> 100.4; 101.3; 102.5; 103.6,8; 104.1; 105.1,5,6; 106.1,3,4; 107.1 "In each instance the materials quoted derive from written gospels, usually from Matthew and Luke, in one instance from Mark, and each time the term serves to quote, or to refer to, gospel materials which demonstrate that the prophecy of the Psalm has been fulfilled in the story of Jesus.  The "memoirs of the apostles" are used as reliable historical records." p40 – "Justin uses the term gospel only three times <i>1 Apol.</i> 66.3, <i>Dial.</i> 10.2; 100.1."  p.&#160;41 – "It is evident that "gospel" refers to the same literature that Justin otherwise calls "memoirs of the apostles".  The use of the plural in <i>1 Apol.</i> 66.3 indicates that Justin knew of more than one written gospel."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._36–37,43-54"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._36–37,43_54-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester 1990 <i>Ancient Christian Gospels: Their History and Development</i> pp.&#160;36–37,43; pp.&#160;36–37 – "...there is no evidence that anyone before Marcion had used the term "gospel" as a designation for a written document.&#160;...those writings of Justin which are preserved, his two <i>Apologies</i> and his <i>Dialogue with Trypho</i>, clearly show the effects of Marcion's challenge." p.&#160;43 – "In direct antithesis to Marcion's use of the written gospel, Justin binds these gospels to the prophetic revelation in the Old Testament scriptures."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Aune_1987_p._67-55"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Aune_1987_p._67_55-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Aune (1987) <i>The New Testament in its Literary Environment</i> p.&#160;67 – "Justin Martyr (writing ca. 155) described the Gospels as 'reminiscences [apomnemoneumata] of the apostles' (1 Apology 66.3; 67.3) and 'reminiscences of Peter' (Dialogue with Trypho 106.3). Thus Justin, like Matthew, Luke, and Papias, prefers to designate the Gospels by a recognized literary form.  Though <i>apomnemoneumata</i> are not carefully defined in rhetorical handbooks, they are essentially expanded chreiai, i.e., sayings and/or actions of or about specific individuals, set in a narrative framework and transmitted by <i>memory</i> (hence "reliable").&#160;... His use of the term "reminiscences", therefore, suggests a connection to Xenophon's <i>Memorabilia</i> (in Greek <i>apomnemoneumata</i>), a "biography" of Socrates."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._33–34,38–40-56"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._33–34,38–40_56-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester 1990 <i>Ancient Christian Gospels: Their History and Development</i> pp.&#160;33–34,38–40; pp.&#160;33–34 – "What Papias says about Mark reflects the use of categories which are drawn from the oral tradition.&#160;... The written gospels' authority is assured by the same technical terms which had been established for the oral tradition.&#160;... The term "remember" (mnemoneuein/apomnemoneuein) was decisive for the trustworthiness of the oral tradition." pp.&#160;39–40 – "The composite form of the verb "to remember" (apomnemoneuein) had been used by Papias of Hierapolis as a technical term for the transmission of oral materials about Jesus. If Justin's term "memoirs of the apostles" is derived from this usage, it designates the written gospels as the true recollections of the apostles, trustworthy and accurate, and more reliable than any oral tradition which they are destined to replace."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_p._377-57"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._377_57-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester 1990 <i>Ancient Christian Gospels: Their History and Development</i> p.&#160;377 – "The Christian proclamation about Jesus as Son of God, however, is true (in contrast to pagan myths), because the Christians possess trustworthy historical documents – "remembrances of the apostles" – from which it can be shown that everything in Christ's appearance and work happened in complete agreement with prophecy.  What is demonstrated to be true is the Christian kerygma, not the story of the gospels.  The reports contained in the gospels are used to show that the facts about Christ which the kerygma proclaims happened in complete agreement with the prophecy that announced them."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_p._41-58"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._41_58-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester 1990 <i>Ancient Christian Gospels: Their History and Development</i> p.&#160;41 – "These gospels for Justin possess the authority of written records.  Although they are read in the service of the church, they are not "Holy Scripture" like the law and the prophets."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Hill_2004-59"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Hill_2004_59-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Hill (2004) pp.&#160;345–46; p.&#160;345 – "It is commonly held that in Rome of Justin's day even the Memoirs themselves possessed only a quite limited authority."; p.&#160;346 – He sees in Justin "a parity of authority between these two groups of writings".</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_pp._130,163-60"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._130,163_60-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._130,163_60-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text">Skarsaune (1987) <i>The Proof From Prophecy</i> pp.&#160;130,163; p.&#160;130 – "Justin sometimes had direct access to Matthew and quotes OT texts directly from him.&#160;... (The direct borrowings are most frequent in the <i>Dialogue</i>; in the <i>Apology</i>, Mic 5:1 in <i>1 Apol</i>. 34:1 may be the only instance.)" p.&#160;163 note: Diagram of the internal structure of the putative "kerygma source", showing the insertion of scriptural quotation of Mic 5:1 from Mt. 2:6</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_2000_p._344-61"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-Koester_2000_p._344_61-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Koester_2000_p._344_61-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester, (2000) <i>Introduction to the New Testament: History and literature of Early Christianity.</i> 2nd ed., 1982 1st ed., p.&#160;344 – "On the basis of the gospel quotations of the First Apology and the Dialogue with Trypho, one can conclude with great certainty that Justin also had composed a harmony of the Gospels of Matthew, Mark, and Luke (he did not know the Gospel of John), which is lost but was used by his student Tatian for the composition of his famous and influential four-gospel harmony known as the Diatessaron."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._360–361-62"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._360–361_62-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancient Christian Gospels</i> pp.&#160;360–361; p.&#160;360 – "He knew and quoted especially the Gospels of Matthew and Luke; he must have known the Gospel of Mark as well, though there is only one explicit reference to this Gospel (<i>Dial</i>. 106.3); he apparently had no knowledge of the Gospel of John." footnote #2: "The only possible reference to the Gospel of John is the quotation of a saying in <i>1 Apol</i>. 61.4.."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Westcott_1875_p._120-63"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Westcott_1875_p._120_63-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Westcott (1875) <i>A general survey of the canon of the New Testament</i>, p.&#160;120 – "To quote prophecy habitually without mentioning the Prophet's name would be to deprive it of half its value; and if it seem strange that Justin does not quote the Evangelists like Prophets, it is no less worthy of notice that he does quote by name the single prophetic book of the New Testament.&#160;... This reference to the Apocalypse appears to illustrate the difference which Justin makes between his quotations from the Prophecies and the Gospels."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Bonwetsch_1914_p._284-64"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Bonwetsch_1914_p._284_64-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Bonwetsch (1914) <i>New Schaff–Herzog Encyclopedia of Religious Knowledge</i>, p.&#160;284; Also see, <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Ante-Nicene_Fathers/Volume_I/JUSTIN_MARTYR/Introductory_Note_to_the_Martyrdom_of_Justin_Martyr" class="extiw" title="s:Ante-Nicene Fathers/Volume I/JUSTIN MARTYR/Introductory Note to the Martyrdom of Justin Martyr">Martyrdom of Justin Martyr</a> at Wikisource</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Skarsaune_2007_pp._380–381-65"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Skarsaune_2007_pp._380–381_65-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Skarsaune (2007) <i>Jewish Believers in Jesus</i> pp.&#160;380–81</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._382–383-66"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._382–383_66-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancient Christian Gospels</i> pp.&#160;382–383 – "In the discussion of the prophecy for the place of Jesus' birth (<i>1 Apology</i> 34), Justin only quotes the prophecy of Micah 5:1 and then remarks that Jesus was born in this 'village in the land of Judah which is 35 stades from Jerusalem' (<i>1 Apol</i>. 34:2).  No actual narrative material from a gospel is quoted.&#160;... However, the quotation of the text of Micah 5:1 is not given in the text of the LXX; rather, Justin follows the form of the text quoted in Matt. 2:6.&#160;... The form of the quotation that appears in Matt 2:6 departs considerably from both the LXX and the Hebrew text.  It is, in fact, a combination of Micah 5:1 and 2 Sam 5:2; only the latter speaks of the prince's function as the Shepard of Israel.  The conflated quotation was wholly the work of Matthew.  There can be no question that Justin is quoting this Matthean text."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_p._365-67"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._365_67-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancient Christian Gospels</i> p.&#160;365 – "The vast majority of the sayings quoted in Justin's writings are harmonizations of the texts of Matthew and Luke.  These harmonizations are not casual or accidental, but systematic and consistent, (this certainly excludes...careless quotation from memory as an explanation for Justin's harmonizations) and they involve the composition of longer sections of parallel sayings from both gospels."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Bellinzoni_1967_p._141-68"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Bellinzoni_1967_p._141_68-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Bellinzoni (1967) <i>Sayings of Jesus in Justin Martyr</i> p.&#160;141 – "It must, however, be emphasized that there is absolutely no evidence that Justin ever composed a complete harmony of the synoptic gospels; his harmonies were of limited scope and were apparently composed for didactic purposes.  Whether the thought of a full gospel harmony ever occurred to Justin can only be conjectured, but he apparently never undertook to compose such a work."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_p._370-69"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._370_69-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>The Ancient Christian Gospels</i> p.&#160;370 footnote 2: "Bellinzoni (<i>Sayings of Jesus in Justin Martyr</i> p.&#160;100) collapses stage (1) [a systematic harmonization of the texts of Matthew and Luke] and (2) [the composition of a cluster of sayings that warn against false prophets] of this process. He assumes that the harmonizations were made specifically for the composition of a catechism.  This assumption, however, cannot explain why also the narrative materials quoted by Justin were drawn from a harmonized gospel text."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_p._378-70"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._378_70-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancient Christian Gospels</i> p.&#160;378 – "The question is whether Justin composed these harmonizations and inserted additional phrases just for the purpose of his demonstration of scriptural proof or whether he drew on a written gospel text that was already harmonized and expanded.  It seems to me that we are not witnessing the work of an apologist who randomly selects pieces of various gospels and invents additional phrases for the purpose of a tight argument of literal fulfillment of scripture; nor can one solve the complex problems of Justin's quotations of gospel narrative materials by the hypothesis of a ready-made, established text of a harmonized gospel as his source.  Rather, his writings permit insights into a school of scriptural exegesis in which careful comparison of written gospels with the prophecies of scripture endeavored to produce an even more comprehensive new gospel text."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_p._145-71"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_p._145_71-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Skarsaune (1987) <i>The Proof From Prophecy</i> p.&#160;145 – "<i>1 Apol</i>. 33 contains an elaborate explanation of Is 7:14.&#160;... One notices that the fulfillment report is stylized so as to match the prophecy perfectly.  That Justin did not entirely formulate it <i>ad hoc</i> is demonstrated by the close parallel in the <i>Proteuangelium Iakobi</i> (PJ 11:3), where much of the same combination of Matthean and Lukan elements occurs.  Probably all three elements (Prophecy – Exposition – Fulfillment report) were present in Justin's source.  And – as pointed out by Koester [Koester (1956) p.&#160;67] – it seems the same source is employed once more in <i>Dial</i>. 84."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_p._379-72"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._379_72-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancient Christian Gospels</i> p.&#160;379 – "<i>1 Apol</i>. 33 gives as proof concerning Jesus' birth the prophecy of Isa 7:14.  The text of this scriptural passage is presented in a form that is influenced by its quotation in Matt 1:23."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_pp._32–34-73"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._32–34_73-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Skarsaune (1987) <i>The Proof From Prophecy</i> pp.&#160;32–34; p.&#160;32 – "It is obvious that Justin's quotation of IS 7:14 in <i>1 Apol</i>. 33:1 has Mt 1:23 as its direct or indirect source.  There are indications in the context which indicate that we should reckon with an intermediary source between Mt and Justin.  This intermediary source may account for the deviations from Matthew's text." p.&#160;33 – Diagram of Mt 1:23, Is 7:14 LXX, and <i>1 Apol</i>. 33:1  p.&#160;34 – "To conclude: Although Is 7:14 has its peculiar problems in Justin,&#160;... we have found confirmation for our thesis concerning Justin and his 'testimony sources': Justin claims the text from Mt 1:23 – probably transmitted through an intermediary source – as the true LXX."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._380–81-74"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._380–81_74-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancient Christian Gospels</i> pp.&#160;380–81 – "The text of <i>1 Apol</i>. 33:5 is a harmony of two angelic announcements, the one from Matthew in which the angel calls Joseph in a dream, the other from Luke's narrative of the annunciation.  While the passage begins with a sentence from Luke, 'from the Holy Spirit' is interpolated from Matt 1:20.  The naming of Jesus and the reason for this name is given according to Matt 1:21.&#160;... But in order to argue for the fulfillment of Isa 7:14 in <i>1 Apol</i>. 33:3–6, the report of the command to name the child 'Jesus' did not need to refer to the Matthean form.&#160;... It is evident, therefore, that Justin is quoting from a harmonized gospel text... Justin's gospel text must have continued with the remainder of the Lukan pericope of the annunciation.  In the introduction to the harmonization of Luke 1:31–32 and Matt 1:20–21, Justin had already alluded to the Lukan continuation of the story: <i>1 Apol</i>. 33:4&#160;... recalls Luke 1:35 ("The Holy Spirit will come upon you and the power of the Most High will overshadow you.")</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_pp._143,425-75"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._143,425_75-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Skarsaune (1987) <i>The Proof From Prophecy</i> pp.&#160;143,425; p.&#160;143 – "Taking as a working hypothesis that Justin in <i>1 Apol</i>. 32/35 and <i>Dial</i>. 52–54 is using a source containing OT prophecies, expositions and fulfillment reports, it is easy to recognize the different procedure in the <i>Apology</i> and the <i>Dialogue</i>.  In the <i>Apology</i>, Justin reproduces the source rather faithfully, only rearranging the material... In the <i>Dialogue</i> Justin is much more independent in his handling of his (kerygma) source.  He has turned to the primary sources behind the testimony source, that is, he has turned to the LXX and Matthew." p.&#160;425 – "The prooftexts themselves were presented in a free, targumizing version of the standard LXX text, closely adapted to Christian exegesis and polemic concerns.&#160;... Justin may have become heir to <i>Schriftbeweistraktate</i> which were part of a school tradition.  These tracts probably also comprised brief fulfillment reports.  We encounter this tradition of texts and exposition in its purest form in <i>1 Apol</i>. 31–53.  Here Justin is still almost entirely dependent on the received texts and the adjacent exegesis.&#160;... Justin's main modification is a rearrangement within the series, motivated by Justin's fear that his readers might not recognize some of his prooftexts as real prophecies."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Skarsaune_2007_pp._381–85-76"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Skarsaune_2007_pp._381–85_76-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Skarsaune (2007) <i>Jewish Believers in Jesus</i> pp.&#160;381–85; p.&#160;381 – "The reason I have called this hypothetical source the "kerygma source" is twofold.  First, it share some striking parallels with the lost writing <i>The Kerygma of Peter</i> (ca. 125) of which a few fragments are quoted in Clement of Alexandria.  Second, it seems to have had a creed-like enumeration of Jesus' messianic career, a christological "kerygma", as its basic structure."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_pp._154–56-77"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._154–56_77-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Skarsaune (1987) <i>The Proof From Prophecy</i> pp.&#160;154–56; p.&#160;156 – "In the <i>Apology</i>, the idea is the following: Since the prophecies covering the first coming of Christ can be shown to have been fulfilled in great detail, we may safely conclude that those prophecies which predict His glorious second coming will also be fulfilled."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Skarsaune_2007_pp._388–9-78"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Skarsaune_2007_pp._388–9_78-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Skarsaune (2007) <i>Jewish Believers in Jesus</i> pp.&#160;388–9 – "The Christology is clearly messianic in function: the 'Son of God' concept is demonstrated functionally as the Messiah being enthroned at God's right hand, ruling, and coming to judge the living and the dead, thus acting in a divine role.  On the whole, this Christology is very close to that of Matthew, but also to the Christology of Justin's source in <i>1 Apol</i>. 31–53."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_p._198-79"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_p._198_79-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Skarsaune (1987) <i>The Proof From Prophecy</i> pp.&#160;197–198,391–392; p.&#160;197 – "Justin's narrative is a harmonization of the Synoptic accounts.  There are other non-synoptic details in the context, however, which may indicate a non-synoptic source besides the Synoptic Gospels." pp.&#160;391–392 – "I have argued above that the narrative of Jesus' baptism in <i>Dial</i>. 88:3 derives from the "recapitulation" source.&#160;... Men believed that Jesus was the son of Joseph, but the heavenly voice proclaimed him as God's son.  Perhaps the mention of the fire is related to this idea: It may have been conceived of as a purifying or testing fire.&#160;... Jesus at his baptism was tested as God's son by the fire, but not made God's son at his baptism.  This, I gather, is also the idea embodied in Justin's narrative: Jesus was not made or established as God's son in his baptism, but he was proved to be God's son – proved by testing, or by conquering the fire."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_pp._222–23,238,383–84,393-80"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._222–23,238,383–84,393_80-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Skarsaune (1987) <i>The Proof From Prophecy</i> pp.&#160;222–23,238,383–84,393; p.&#160;384 – "In the temptation story, Christ as the Son of God, the second Adam, is tested.  The temptation follows immediately after the heavenly voice has proclaimed 'Thou art my son...'.  This is especially clear in <i>Dial</i>. 103:5f.&#160;... The special relevance of this passage is that it proves how deeply the recapitulation idea is integrated into Justin's inherited material.  The etymology given for Satanas has a special function: It proves that the 'Satanas' encountered by Jesus in his temptation was the same as the 'serpent' encountered by Adam – Satanas means 'apostate serpent', i.e. the serpent of Gen. 3.  In other words: Jesus met the same adversary as the first Adam." p.&#160;393 – "It is interesting to notice that only two Semitic etymologies provided by Justin both refer to the temptation story: 'Satanas' and 'Israel' (<i>Dial</i>. 103:5 and <i>Dial</i>. 125:4) – and as we have seen already, they presuppose a harmonistic version of the temptation story which is not created <i>ad hoc</i> by Justin.  The gist of the whole material is succinctly summarized in <i>Dial</i>. 103:6: As the devil led Adam astray, he thought he could seduce the second Adam also."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._394–395-81"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._394–395_81-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancients Christians Gospels</i> pp.&#160;394–395 – "In <i>Dial</i>. 88, Justin twice reports the coming of the holy spirit upon Jesus at his baptism.  He gives this report in order to demonstrate the fulfillment of the prophecies of Isa 11:1–3 and Joel 2:28–29 about the coming of the spirit which he had quoted in <i>Dial</i>. 87:2 and 6.&#160;... Finally, the heavenly voice is given by Justin in a citation of Ps. 2:7, while Mark and Matthew present a wording of the heavenly voice which is a conflation of Isa 42:1 and 44:2.  Only the Western text of Luke 3:22 presents the heavenly voice in the form that must be presupposed for Justin's source.  Justin cannot have been the author of this form of the heavenly voice; he had no special interest in proving the fulfillment of this scriptural text, although he is quite aware of its appearance in scripture as a word of David, i.e., a psalm that David wrote.  That Justin's source already contained this form of the heavenly voice is confirmed in <i>Dial</i>. 103:6, where he refers to it once more in passing; introducing a remark about Jesus' temptation, he again quotes the exact text of Luke 3:22 D = Ps. 2:7."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_p._395-82"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._395_82-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancients Christians Gospels</i> p.&#160;395 – "In order to prove the fulfillment of the prophecies of Isa 11:1–3 and Joel 2:28–29, Justin only had to report the coming of the spirit upon Jesus.  But not only does he add the report about the heavenly voice, he also mentions 'that a fire was lit in the Jordan'.  Nothing in the context of Justin's discussion requires a mention of this phenomenon.  It must have been part of the text Justin was quoting."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Rokeah_2002_pp._20–21-83"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Rokeah_2002_pp._20–21_83-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Rokeah (2002) <i>Justin Martyr and the Jews</i> pp.&#160;20–21 – "The accepted view is that Justin did not know Hebrew.  There is clear-cut and overwhelming evidence for Justin's absolute reliance upon the Septuagint.  The explanation for any apparent acquaintance or knowledge of Hebrew in Justin's writings should be sought elsewhere: in his sources.&#160;... <i>Dial</i>. 103:5 contains the only two Hebrew–Aramaic etymologies in the entire work: of <i>satan</i>, and of <i>yisrael</i>.  The source of these is apparently the work of Aristo of Pella, <i>The Altercation of Jason and Papiscus</i>."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Skarsaune_2007_pp._399–400-84"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Skarsaune_2007_pp._399–400_84-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Skarsaune (2007) <i>Jewish Believers in Jesus</i> pp.&#160;399–400; "In Justin's source, the Messiah is presented as God's preexistent Wisdom who has descended to earth, and ascended again to his heavenly glory.&#160;... Here I add another aspect of great significance in Justin's source, namely that Jesus is portrayed as the second and anti-typical Adam.  He reverses the fall of Adam by conquering where Adam was conquered.  He "recapitulates" in his own story the story of Adam, but with the opposite point of departure, the opposite direction and the opposite result.&#160;... The very point of the (pseudo-)etymology given for Satanas in this passage is to identify the Tempter addressed by Jesus in Matt 4:11 (conflated with Matt 16:23) with the serpent that tempted the first man.  In this way the parallelism between the first and second Adam is made plain.  Since Justin knew no Hebrew and probably no Aramaic, there is every reason to think he got this midrashic etymology from a source..."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_p._361-85"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._361_85-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancient Christian Gospels</i> p.&#160;361 – "The most striking feature is that these sayings exhibit many harmonizations of the text of Matthew and Luke.  However, the simple assumption of a harmonized gospel cannot explain all the peculiarities of the quotations."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._99–100-86"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._99–100_86-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Bellinzoni (1967) <i>Sayings of Jesus in Justin Martyr</i> pp.&#160;99–100 – "It has already been argued above that the entire section <i>Apol</i>. 15–17 may have been based on a single source different from the sources underlying the rest of Justin's sayings of Jesus, and I have tried to indicate that this section has many features in common with primitive Christian catechisms."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._64–67-87"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._64–67_87-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Bellinzoni (1967) <i>Sayings of Jesus in Justin Martyr</i> pp.&#160;64–67; p.&#160;66 – "the form of the saying in James is a more simple paranetic form than the text of Matthew, where each example is elaborated and where the command is not what one should do but what one should say. It, therefore, appears that the form of the saying in Jas. 5:12 is older than Matthew's version.&#160;... This evidence would seem to indicate that <i>Apol</i>. 16:5 was here based on the text of Mt. 5:34,37 that had either been harmonized in part with Jas. 5:12 or with the parenetic tradition that underlies Jas. 5:12.  The evidence of several of the fathers indicates a widespread knowledge of a text similar to <i>Apol</i>. 16:5." (Clem. of Alex. <i>Strom</i>. V 14,99; Clem. of Alex. <i>Strom</i>. VII 11,67; Cyril of Alex. <i>De Ador. et Verit. VI; Eusebius </i>Dem. Ev.<i> III 3,13; Eusebius </i>Comm. in Ps. 14<i> 4; Epiphanius </i>Adv. Her. XIX 6,21; Gregory of Nyssa <i>In Cant. of Cant.</i> Homily XIII)</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_p._363-88"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._363_88-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancient Christian Gospels</i> p.&#160;363 – "Thus...it is not likely that Justin is quoting from the text of Matthew but from a catechism, whose text was influenced by the formulation preserved in Jas 5:12 but not necessarily dependent upon the Epistle of James."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._356,365–67-89"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._356,365–67_89-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancient Christian Gospels</i> pp.&#160;356,365–67; p.&#160;367 – "The method of harmonization includes two different procedures: (1) whenever the texts of Matthew and Luke are closely parallel, either the Matthean or the Lukan phrase or a conflation of both is chosen; (2) whenever the texts of Matthew and Luke differ considerably, as in Matt 7:22 and Luke 13:26, major portions of the two texts are combined; thus, one finds Luke's 'we were eating and drinking' as well as Matthew's 'we prophesied etc.'."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._22–25-90"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._22–25_90-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Bellinzoni (1967) <i>Sayings of Jesus in Justin Martyr</i> pp.&#160;22–25; pp.&#160;24–25 – "These consistent features of harmonization found in <i>Apol</i>. 16:11 and <i>Dial</i>. 76:5 leave little doubt that Justin used as a source for these passages a written harmony of Mt. 7:22f and Lk. 13:26f, and this harmonization of Matthew and Luke is further evident in several of the early fathers quoted in the texts below.&#160;... A comparison of this harmonization of Matthew and Luke in the patristic quotations leaves little doubt that Justin used a harmony of Mt. 7:22f and Lk. 13:26f and that this harmony was known to other fathers in substantially the same form as that used by Justin (Origen <i>Contra Celsum</i> II 49; Origen <i>Ev. Jo.</i> XXXII 8,11; Pamphilius <i>Apol. pro Orig.</i> V). Further, the witness of <i>2 Clement</i> here proves the existence of this harmonization of Matthew and Luke previous to Justin."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._98–99-91"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Bellinzoni_1967_pp._98–99_91-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Bellinzoni (1967) <i>Sayings of Jesus in Justin Martyr</i> pp.&#160;98–99; p.&#160;99 – "Therefore we can conclude with certainty that these five verses are based on a source that was a carefully composed harmony of material from Matthew and Luke and that was based on the order of Matthew 7."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_pp._367–370-92"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_pp._367–370_92-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancient Christian Gospels</i> pp.&#160;367–370; p.&#160;369 – "This section of Justin's quotation of Jesus' sayings rests on deliberate and careful composition of the parallel texts of Matthew and Luke, but is also disrupted by interpolations from different contexts." p.&#160;370 – "Thus Justin himself did not compose this cluster of sayings for this particular context.  He use an already existing collection."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Bellinzoni_1967_p._100-93"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Bellinzoni_1967_p._100_93-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Bellinzoni (1967) <i>Sayings of Jesus in Justin Martyr</i> p.&#160;100 – "It is, therefore, quite probable from the foregoing discussion that there is underlying <i>Apol</i>. 15–17 a primitive Christian catechism in use in Justin's school in Rome, a catechism that was known in similar form to Clement of Alexandria, Origen, and the author of the Pseudo-Clementine <i>Homilies</i>, a catechism based primarily on the text of the Sermon on the Mount but that harmonized related material from Mark, Luke, and from other parts of Matthew, and a catechism whose tradition was of great influence in later manuscript witnesses of the synoptic gospels."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Koester_1990_p._375-94"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Koester_1990_p._375_94-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Koester (1990) <i>Ancient Christian Gospels</i> p.&#160;375 – "The catechetical character of these clusters of sayings is evident in their usage by Justin&#160;... It is difficult to determine in each instance the degree to which Justin has supplemented and rearranged these collections. But it appears that the catechetical collections already existed and that Justin himself did not compose them."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Skarsaune_1987_pp._52–53,148–150,431-95"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Skarsaune_1987_pp._52–53,148–150,431_95-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Skarsaune (1987) <i>The Proof From Prophecy</i> pp.&#160;52–53,148–150,431; p.&#160;150 – "This tract must have had a somewhat other orientation than the source employed by Justin in <i>1 Apol</i>. 32–35.  It was not concerned with a prophecy–fulfillment scheme, but with correspondence between OT texts and Greek mythology."  p.&#160;53 – "It is unlikely that it (the text in <i>1 Apol</i>. 60:9 introduced as a prophecy of Moses) ever occurred in a Bible text...it is more likely that Justin took it from the source which also provided him with the (harmonistic) 'citations' from Plato in A 60.&#160;... In this case we have reason to suspect a tractate of some kind, which included Plato quotations as well." p.&#160;431 – "It remains to be remarked that Justin also has made other additions from sources containing OT material, but these are strictly speaking not parts of the scriptural proof.  In <i>1 Apol</i>. 54f and <i>Dial</i>. 69f Justin has added material from a source which was occupied with demonic imitations of OT Messianic prophecies, and in <i>1 Apol</i>. 59f he has a little tract on philosophic borrowings from Moses.  One should not exclude the possibility that these two blocks of material derive from the same source, which might well be an earlier Christian <i>Apology</i>."</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-96"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-96">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Philippe Bobichon, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.academia.edu/20441884/Composite_Citations_and_Textual_Composition_in_Justin_Martyr_Draft_">"Composite Citations and Textual Composition in Justin Martyr"</a> in S.
